					<description><![CDATA[<p>Researchers at Materials Center Leoben, PVA TePla, and Advanced Semiconductor Engineering (ASE) published “AI-Based Image Enhancement Improves Detection of Interconnect Defects in Microelectronic Packaging.” Abstract Excerpt: &#8220;Traditional failure-analysis (FA) methods face growing challenges due to the increasing miniaturization and complexity of modern semiconductor devices. In particular, the detection of cracks in the extreme low K... <a class="read_more" href="https://semiengineering.com/ai-image-enhancement-improves-detection-of-flip-chip-interconnect-defects-mcl-pva-tepla-ase/">&#187; read more</a></p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/ai-image-enhancement-improves-detection-of-flip-chip-interconnect-defects-mcl-pva-tepla-ase/">AI Image Enhancement Improves Detection Of Flip-Chip Interconnect Defects (MCL, PVA TePla, ASE)</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Researchers at <strong>Materials Center Leoben, PVA TePla</strong>, and <strong>Advanced Semiconductor Engineering</strong> (<strong>ASE)</strong> published “AI-Based Image Enhancement Improves Detection of Interconnect Defects in Microelectronic Packaging.”</p>
<p><strong>Abstract Excerpt</strong>:<br />
&#8220;Traditional failure-analysis (FA) methods face growing challenges due to the increasing miniaturization and complexity of modern semiconductor devices. In particular, the detection of cracks in the extreme low K layers of flip-chips, utilized in advanced packaging architectures at the back end of line (BEOL), is challenging. To improve detectability of these so-called “white bumps”, we propose an AI-based image-enhancement workflow, specifically tailored to improve scanning acoustic microscopy (SAM) images.&#8221;</p>
<p>Find the <a href="https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s11668-026-02537-z">technical paper here</a>. October 2026</p>
<p>Wilhelmer, R., Djuric-Rissner, T., Czurratis, P. et al. AI-Based Image Enhancement Improves Detection of Interconnect Defects in Microelectronic Packaging. J Fail. Anal. and Preven. (2026). https://doi.org/10.1007/s11668-026-02537-z. <a href="https://s100.copyright.com/AppDispatchServlet?title=AI-Based%20Image%20Enhancement%20Improves%20Detection%20of%20Interconnect%20Defects%20in%20Microelectronic%20Packaging&amp;author=Raphael%20Wilhelmer%20et%20al&amp;contentID=10.1007%2Fs11668-026-02537-z&amp;copyright=The%20Author%28s%29&amp;publication=1547-7029&amp;publicationDate=2026-10-05&amp;publisherName=SpringerNature&amp;orderBeanReset=true&amp;oa=CC%20BY">Open access</a>.</p>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Researchers at imec and Vrije Universiteit Brussels published “Embodied Emissions of 2.5D and 3D Scaling in Chipletized GPUs for Low-Carbon AI.” Abstract &#8220;GPU compute resources are scaling to support growing AI workloads, leading to further rises in the climate change impact of hyperscale data centers that rely on high-performance GPUs. To model how packaging decisions... <a class="read_more" href="https://semiengineering.com/3d-integration-reduces-embodied-emissions-impact-of-gpu-scaling-imec-vub/">&#187; read more</a></p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/3d-integration-reduces-embodied-emissions-impact-of-gpu-scaling-imec-vub/">3D Integration Reduces Embodied-Emissions Impact Of GPU Scaling (imec, VUB)</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Researchers at <strong>imec</strong> and <strong>Vrije Universiteit Brussels</strong> published “Embodied Emissions of 2.5D and 3D Scaling in Chipletized GPUs for Low-Carbon AI.”</p>
<p><strong>Abstract</strong><br />
&#8220;GPU compute resources are scaling to support growing AI workloads, leading to further rises in the climate change impact of hyperscale data centers that rely on high-performance GPUs. To model how packaging decisions impact the carbon footprint we have estimated the embodied emissions (EE) of a chiplet-based GPU that has been scaled in 2.5D and 3D using the imec netzero modeling platform. We doubled the number of compute units and high bandwidth memory (HBM) capacity in the 2.5D and 3D scaled systems and found that 3D scaling results in an 89% increase in EE, as compared to an increase of 141% for 2.5D scaling, positioning 3D scaling as an attractive solution to limit the embodied emissions due to GPU scaling.&#8221;</p>
<p>Find the <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32830">technical paper here</a>. October 2026</p>
<p>Watson, Hannah, Arjun Singh, Jennifer Stephenson, Job Soethoudt, Jonas Svedas, Debjyoti Bhattacharjee, and James Myers. &#8220;Embodied Emissions of 2.5D and 3D Scaling in Chipletized GPUs for Low-Carbon AI.&#8221; arXiv preprint arXiv:2610.32830 (2026).</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/3d-integration-reduces-embodied-emissions-impact-of-gpu-scaling-imec-vub/">3D Integration Reduces Embodied-Emissions Impact Of GPU Scaling (imec, VUB)</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>

]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Researchers at <strong>IBM Research</strong> published “Meter-scale quantum interconnects between large quantum processors.”</p>
<p><strong>Abstract Excerpt</strong>:<br />
&#8220;Modular quantum architecture requires long-distance quantum interconnects to stitch together individual processor modules. Such interconnects, long-distance couplers or L-couplers, must support coherent quantum operation over meter-scale distances and scale to multiple connections between any two modules. Here we demonstrate four L-couplers operating between two 120-qubit IBM Nighthawk R1 superconducting qubit processors. &#8220;</p>
<p>Find the <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29986">technical paper here</a>. October 2026. IBM&#8217;s related blog post is <a href="https://www.ibm.com/quantum/blog/connecting-quantum-processors-with-l-couplers">here</a>.</p>
<p>Sundaresan, Neereja M., et al. &#8220;Meter-scale quantum interconnects between large quantum processors.&#8221; arXiv preprint arXiv:2610.29986 (2026).</p>

		<title>Hardware Defense Blocks Memory-Dependent Prefetcher Leakage (KU Leuven)</title>

		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Technical Paper Link]]></dc:creator>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Researchers at KU Leuven and EURECOM published “SecDMP: Provably Secure Data Memory-Dependent Prefetching.” Abstract Excerpt: &#8220;In this work, we address this gap by designing a defense that is both secure and efficient. First, we propose a classification of DMP leakage, categorize existing and novel attacks, and analyze which leakage classes are mitigated by existing defenses.... <a class="read_more" href="https://semiengineering.com/hardware-defense-blocks-memory-dependent-prefetcher-leakage-ku-leuven/">&#187; read more</a></p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/hardware-defense-blocks-memory-dependent-prefetcher-leakage-ku-leuven/">Hardware Defense Blocks Memory-Dependent Prefetcher Leakage (KU Leuven)</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Researchers at <strong>KU Leuven</strong> and <strong>EURECOM</strong> published “SecDMP: Provably Secure Data Memory-Dependent Prefetching.”</p>
<p><strong>Abstract Excerpt</strong>:<br />
&#8220;In this work, we address this gap by designing a defense that is both secure and efficient. First, we propose a classification of DMP leakage, categorize existing and novel attacks, and analyze which leakage classes are mitigated by existing defenses. We validate this analysis empirically by implementing the first open-source DMPs on a RISC-V processor, showing that the only existing defense that aims to preserve DMP performance fails to protect against entire leakage classes. Next, we propose SecDMP, a provably secure and efficient hardware-based defense. SecDMP requires secrets to be marked in memory and ensures that DMPs never encode these secrets in the microarchitecture&#8212;without compromising performance for public data. We formally prove that SecDMP introduces no leakage beyond a baseline semantics without DMPs. Notably, when combined with speculative-execution defenses, SecDMP restores complete microarchitectural security for constant-time code. Finally, our empirical evaluation shows that SecDMP prevents all DMP leakage while incurring minimal performance overhead and hardware cost.&#8221;</p>
<p>Find the <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37454">technical paper here</a>. October 2026</p>
<p>Sprengers, Jens, Marton Bognar, Lesly-Ann Daniel, and Frank Piessens. &#8220;SecDMP: Provably Secure Data Memory-Dependent Prefetching (Extended Version).&#8221; arXiv preprint arXiv:2610.37454 (2026). <a href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/">License</a>: CC BY-SA 4.0</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/hardware-defense-blocks-memory-dependent-prefetcher-leakage-ku-leuven/">Hardware Defense Blocks Memory-Dependent Prefetcher Leakage (KU Leuven)</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>

		<title>Co-Optimizing Access Points Improves Analog Placement (UT Austin)</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/co-optimizing-access-points-improves-analog-placement-ut-austin/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/co-optimizing-access-points-improves-analog-placement-ut-austin/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Technical Paper Link]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 02 Nov 2026 18:57:45 +0000</pubDate>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Researchers at the University of Texas at Austin published “APPlace: Co-Optimizing Device Locations and Access Points for Routing-Aware Analog Placement.” Abstract &#8220;Analog layout automation remains challenging in Analog/Mixed-Signal circuit design, because parasitics and mismatches introduced during layout strongly affect circuit performance. Placement is especially critical among the layout stages, as it constrains the routing solution... <a class="read_more" href="https://semiengineering.com/co-optimizing-access-points-improves-analog-placement-ut-austin/">&#187; read more</a></p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/co-optimizing-access-points-improves-analog-placement-ut-austin/">Co-Optimizing Access Points Improves Analog Placement (UT Austin)</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Researchers at the University of Texas at Austin published “APPlace: Co-Optimizing Device Locations and Access Points for Routing-Aware Analog Placement.”</p>
<p><strong>Abstract</strong><br />
&#8220;Analog layout automation remains challenging in Analog/Mixed-Signal circuit design, because parasitics and mismatches introduced during layout strongly affect circuit performance. Placement is especially critical among the layout stages, as it constrains the routing solution space and the achievable post-layout performance. Existing analytical placement methods optimize device coordinates under center-based wirelength proxies and leave access point (AP) selection to routing. In analog circuits, however, a routing pin is an extended piece of terminal metal rather than a fixed point, and the best AP on it depends on where the other connected devices are placed, so a coordinate-only placement optimizes for connection points that the router will not use. We present APPlace, a routing-aware analog placement framework that co-optimizes device locations and AP selections during global placement, followed by a legalization stage that preserves the structure established by global placement. Experimental results on four real-world analog benchmarks show that APPlace achieves better overall post-layout performance than representative baselines at comparable layout area. Ablation studies further show that removing AP co-optimization causes up to a 13 dB CMRR loss among the OTA benchmarks and a 23% increase in comparator delay.&#8221;</p>
<p>Find the <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39515">technical paper here</a>. October 2026</p>
<p>Liu, Bingyang, Xiaohan Gao, Souradip Poddar, and David Z. Pan. &#8220;APPlace: Co-Optimizing Device Locations and Access Points for Routing-Aware Analog Placement.&#8221; arXiv preprint arXiv:2610.39515 (2026). <a href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/">License</a>: Attribution 4.0 International.</p>

		<item>
		<title>Shift Left Is Becoming A System-Level Problem</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/shift-left-is-becoming-a-system-level-problem/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/shift-left-is-becoming-a-system-level-problem/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Ed Sperling]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 02 Nov 2026 08:01:30 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Round Tables]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[System-Level Design]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Top Stories]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[agentic AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Axiomise]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[emulation]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[formal verification]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Hiveware]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[shift left]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Silvaco]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[simulation]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Synopsys]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[system-level design]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[validation]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[verification]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283537</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Rise in complexity is pushing engineers to validate hardware, software, and physical constraints earlier in the design flow, but fragmented workflows make that difficult.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/shift-left-is-becoming-a-system-level-problem/">Shift Left Is Becoming A System-Level Problem</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>Key Takeaways:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Shift left is pushing beyond verification to system-level validation.</li>
<li>Fragmented workflows with disconnected tools and models are allowing bugs to escape even though individual components have been verified.</li>
<li>Agentic AI may improve coordination across disciplines, identify bottlenecks, and optimize tool usage, but it cannot replace fundamental processes such as simulation and emulation.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<p><strong>Experts at the Table</strong>: Semiconductor Engineering sat down to discuss why shift left is pushing beyond verification to system-level validation, and where AI can and cannot help, with Frank Schirrmeister, executive director of strategy programs and system solutions at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/synopsys-inc/" data-uw-rm-brl="PR" data-uw-original-href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/synopsys-inc/">Synopsys</a>; Ronen Laviv, vice president of business development and strategic partnerships at Hiveware; Jack Berg, vice president of business development at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/silvaco-inc/" data-uw-rm-brl="PR" data-uw-original-href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/silvaco-inc/">Silvaco</a>; Ashish Darbari, founder and CEO of <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/axiomise/" data-uw-rm-brl="PR" data-uw-original-href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/axiomise/">Axiomise</a>. What follows are excerpts of that conversation. To view part one of this discussion, click <a href="https://semiengineering.com/rethinking-shift-left-in-chip-design/">here</a>.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" fetchpriority="high" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283440" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?resize=1716%2C538&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1716" height="538" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?w=1716&amp;ssl=1 1716w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?resize=300%2C94&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?resize=1024%2C321&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?resize=768%2C241&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?resize=1536%2C482&amp;ssl=1 1536w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-28-at-3.04.41-PM.png?resize=600%2C188&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><br />
<strong>L-R: Silvaco’s Berg; Synopsys’ Schirrmeister; Axiomise’s Darbari; Hiveware’s Laviv.</strong></p>
<p><strong>SE: The focus of shift left in design has largely been about verification. But increasingly it&#8217;s also about validation, right?</strong></p>
<p><strong>Darbari</strong>: Yes, and it&#8217;s influencing huge decisions that could make small companies go bankrupt if they get it wrong. We&#8217;ve seen meetings with architects, designers, UVM leads, emulation leads, FPGA and software leads, and VPs, where they&#8217;re all looking at bug escapes and asking, &#8216;Why did this happen?&#8217; The architect is saying, &#8216;It&#8217;s supposed to be this.&#8217; The designer is saying, &#8216;We didn&#8217;t know that.&#8217; The software team says, &#8216;It&#8217;s supposed to be this.&#8217; And we&#8217;re sitting there as formal verification people thinking about communication and the model not being accurate, and I don&#8217;t know how well we&#8217;ll be able to solve this unless we bring the software into the development cycle.</p>
<p><strong>Schirrmeister</strong>: It&#8217;s not only software. It&#8217;s all the other aspects, like multi-physics. EM, thermal, and mechanical, which all have to be integrated. To look at this differently, there are hurdles that I cannot fix in this project. But perhaps I can fix it in the next one. It&#8217;s a labyrinth. We have models that are digital twins that are irrefutable, because we&#8217;ve measured them against the real thing. We can&#8217;t overcome those. We are dependent on the application. It may be more vertical, it may be more horizontally stacked to fit into a certain form factor, like a phone or a data center. But it&#8217;s like a video game where you have these things popping up. I want to package it this way, but the packaging guy says we can&#8217;t do that, at least not today. Maybe a year from now. All of this needs to be taken into consideration as part of your model.</p>
<p><strong>Dabari</strong>: Absolutely correct.</p>
<p><strong>Laviv</strong>: Shrinking times for packaging or power estimation or simulation, or even emulation — isn&#8217;t that something we could look at? We&#8217;re shifting left using AI in order to do stuff earlier.</p>
<p><strong>Berg</strong>: Verification traditionally has been the longest and most expensive part of doing a design, and it would be nice to say we can take everything and shift it all left. There are chunks that are being shifted left and verified, and that&#8217;s good. But how to shift everything left is something we&#8217;re still learning.</p>
<p><strong>Darbari</strong>: If we pick up historical data from packaging and thermal, why can&#8217;t we start building models to say, &#8216;This happened last time. This time it could be done better.&#8217;</p>
<p><strong>Berg</strong>: You can do that based on inference.</p>
<p><strong>Darbari</strong>: But we don&#8217;t have a tool that ties everything together in one frame. &#8216;This is the model of functional safety, security, power, packaging, thermals. Go build it.&#8217;</p>
<p><strong>Berg</strong>: We have different aspects of that, and we, as an industry, are working slowly but surely to integrate all of those. But we don&#8217;t have a single workflow that does that. And it really is a workflow, not just a single tool.</p>
<p><strong>Laviv</strong>: Nowadays, with AI knowing everything with its skills and touching everything, there is kind of a common database for verification and emulation and all the physical stuff. It might benefit customers if they have access to everything is there to be used, potentially even cross-companies.</p>
<p><strong>Schirrmeister</strong>: There are certain things you can shift left, and others you have to wait for. Certain things are irrefutable. Yes, you can make them faster, but you can&#8217;t bypass them. You can be smarter about how you use them. You can figure out which things should be done in emulation, which should be done in simulation, and which should be done with FPGA-based prototyping. We have customers telling us that if they find something in simulation that should have been found in formal, or simulation beforehand, they&#8217;ll review why that happened.</p>
<p><strong>Laviv</strong>: Why not look at bottlenecks in formal, in verification, in emulation, and see if you can handle those bottlenecks in a way that would open the flow for faster AI?</p>
<p><strong>Schirrmeister</strong>: There are two views here on this whole AI stack. I&#8217;m a firm believer that the underlying portions of the stack will still be required. They will be used in different ways by the AI objects, but you don&#8217;t take away the characterization, the simulation, the emulation. You get smarter about how to use them. But at the end of the day, you need to build the emulator. That takes some time. You need to get the design into it. And there is a certain number of things which you cannot overcome. You can make the tool flow more productive, but the underlying engines still need to execute, and there are limits to how fast they can become.</p>
<p><strong>Darbari</strong>: We need to put all of these things in a singular context because if the manufacturing, or the physics, the heat, or the functional, are all sitting in separate context windows, and the software is sitting somewhere else, then you don&#8217;t have a complete picture of what this whole device will look like. I was coding for one project and I remember asking a designer, &#8216;What would happen to this processor if there was more than one outstanding transaction? Could you discard the later ones?&#8217; And he said, &#8216;We&#8217;ll just discard them.&#8217; Everything was exhaustively proved, and while I was taping, the software teams were running their own stuff. But a bug got missed. The software team had a completely different understanding of how they were going to drive the SoC. So they found a trace, and they said, &#8216;We are losing transactions.&#8217; There was a complete misalignment between what the architect and the designer thought they were building, so they had to patch it at the end of the day. That&#8217;s what worries me now, because things are getting so much more complex. We&#8217;re putting all of this in the hands of an agent, but who is the master at the top level? Is there a master agent?</p>
<p><strong>Schirrmeister</strong>: But there&#8217;s also a counter-example. It&#8217;s true that there might be a communication mismatch, but there also may be complexity issues that are hard to observe by one person, and then your context applies. I have an example from a previous life, when there were software developers and hardware developers, and you had an emulator. The software developers were writing their software, and everything was correct. They talked to each other for days, but then they started pointing fingers. Suddenly, the emulator vendor was at fault. There must be something wrong in the tool. Then we figured out that in the global setting, the code was powered down at that particular state in the system context, and the software developer didn&#8217;t see the register programming as being correct. The hardware developer saw it as correct, but didn&#8217;t know that it switched off because the clock was gated and that area was shut down. From a context standpoint, all that information needs to be communicated. That&#8217;s one issue. But then it also needs to be available in the context of the agentic AI.</p>
<p><strong>Darbari</strong>: Unless we stitch the agents in ways so that we can handle all of these challenges, there will still be bugs creeping through the seams. People will be investing in agents and doing all the stuff you&#8217;re talking about, but there is still no single context where you could look at all of this together. AI could be used for this, and it&#8217;s really what AI should be used for, because it&#8217;s humanly not feasible to know and derive all of this information that should go into a single arrangement.</p>
<p><strong>Laviv</strong>: With chips like [Intel&#8217;s] Broadwell, with 200 billion transistors, we&#8217;ll need much more verification. Today, the tools have challenges running such designs without emulation. We need to see if some things can get shifted left and prepare the simulation environment to pull in the emulation earlier. But if you have such a huge chip, it&#8217;s a challenge. With AI, we&#8217;ve heard how you can interact with the simulation or the API. So if you let AI have some internal insights to formal, to simulation, now you don&#8217;t have to wait two days for a test to end. In the AI era, for each of the tools, we can find hooks that AI can use in order to make better judgments and clean stuff faster.</p>
<p><strong>Darbari</strong>: We&#8217;ve been looking at the power/performance/area estimate, and there are some very interesting insights we&#8217;ve received. We can look at the entire chip, analyze it to figure out if the unused area is burning power. What surprised us most, because we knew we were looking at results where synthesis isn&#8217;t going to remove it, is that we didn&#8217;t have that data. But some of this stuff would be sitting in the die, not getting used. Synthesis isn&#8217;t removing it. Lint isn&#8217;t telling you it&#8217;s bad. Coverage is not telling you it&#8217;s bad. In order for us to be efficient with the end goal, we need to start thinking about the artifacts very early on. So, for example, picking up redundant logic should not be a synthesis problem. This should be a designer&#8217;s problem or an architect&#8217;s problem. If I want to remove a register or retime it, I want to make sure the design is built correctly up front. But whether it is built for the right reason is a separate problem. Maybe that&#8217;s a validation problem. But bringing what used to be a verification or layout problem in as a design or architecture problem is what needs to happen.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/shift-left-is-becoming-a-system-level-problem/">Shift Left Is Becoming A System-Level Problem</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<item>
		<title>Putting AI To The Test</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/putting-ai-to-the-test/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/putting-ai-to-the-test/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Brian Bailey]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 02 Nov 2026 08:01:18 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Perspectives & Analysis]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[System-Level Design]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[What Were They Thinking]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AI-assisted verification]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[ARM]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[ChipAgents]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[EDA]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[LLMs]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[signoff]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[testbench]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[verification]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283522</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Who verifies the verifier? This is the age-old question for anyone involved in the verification of semiconductor systems. Who verifies AI?</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/putting-ai-to-the-test/">Putting AI To The Test</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>There are an increasing number of new EDA companies these days, and this is great news. The industry needs some new vitality, new thought leaders, people willing to take risks. The same goes for established companies that cannot go outside their existing comfort zone while meeting next quarter&#8217;s financial goals. But sometimes, new startups forget the risks associated with semiconductor design. While they may be able to point to an improvement in time to market, or meeting better performance goals, they fail to understand how small that particular step is in the whole process.</p>
<p>Today, a number of companies want us to believe that AI can do everything. Some point the way to automating design, others to verification, and some even claim it can do both at the same time, from the same source material. No, it can&#8217;t. While some of it may be possible in the future, it is far from that point today.</p>
<p>What many of them fail to do is show proof. One example, one design, one piece of IP is not proof. When verifying any new tool or methodology, you always need to hold back a test set to apply at the end. In this case, with AI, it has to be something it has never seen before, never been trained on. That is the only thing that can provide a fair, unbiased result. As soon as you tweak your tool to do well on that test case, it is no longer a true test.</p>
<p>This is why I was delighted to receive communications from Vikash Kumar, senior verification architect at Arm. He has 15+ years of semiconductor industry experience and is the author of the book “AI-Assisted Hardware Verification: Cognitive Verification Architecture with the VEGA Framework.” What he shared with me was his independent work on the quality of existing LLMs for the creation of testbenches.</p>
<p>More than 20 years ago, Certess introduced the industry to mutation analysis. If you inject a bug into the design, does the testbench find it? They used the traditional stuck-at fault model and sophisticated statistical analysis to reduce the total number of faults required. The results were a slap across the face to many companies that thought they were doing a good job, only to find out how poor their testbenches were.</p>
<p>Vikash&#8217;s approach is different. &#8220;The setup was simple — hand AI a spec and the RTL, tell it to verify and report back when it is confident the design is clean, and check what it actually found. I hand-seeded realistic, design-representative functional bugs, which keeps the ground truth clean. I know exactly which bugs were planted, so detection is measured against a known set rather than an impression.&#8221;</p>
<p>I asked Vikash why he chose to insert bugs rather than using the stuck-at model, which has been the industry go-to since the &#8217;80s. &#8220;The whole question with AI verification is whether it comprehends design intent well enough to catch the subtle, intent-level bugs, and that is exactly where it struggled — the misses clustered in data-path, encoding logic, and functional corners, not in anything a stuck-at pattern would have flagged.&#8221;</p>
<p>He started with 25 hand-coded designs spanning bus protocols, DMA engines, ECC memory, control and peripheral logic, and concurrency scenarios. The RTL has never been made public. Into those he inserted 806 bugs. The models were given a specification based on published protocol standards. Three frontier AI models were chosen: Claude Opus 5, GPT-5.4, and GLM-5.2.</p>
<p>&#8220;Without human intervention or predefined time limits, each model generated testbenches, developed coverage goals, executed simulations, identified and corrected RTL defects, and iteratively refined verification until independently declaring sign-off. All defects were seeded upfront, although some became observable only after earlier corrections.&#8221;</p>
<p>Here are his key findings:</p>
<ul>
<li>Claude Opus 5 detected 79.2% of seeded defects, GPT-5.4 detected 76.2%, and GLM-5.2 detected 62.8%.</li>
<li>Even the highest-performing model missed 20.8% of seeded defects, which is approximately 168 bugs.</li>
<li>All three models reached 100% of their self-defined verification goals and declared sign-off despite remaining functional defects.</li>
<li>In this audit, particularly high miss rates were observed in ECC memory, GPIO, and AXI DMA designs.</li>
<li>Some missed defects involved ambiguous specifications. The agents proceeded without requesting clarification and ultimately declared verification complete.</li>
</ul>
<p>Even the best model, Claude Opus 5, did not detect roughly 168 defects. GPT-5.4 missed about 192, and GLM-5.2 missed close to 300. Perhaps even worse is that Vikash ran each model three times, and each run produced different results. But he is very optimistic about the results. &#8220;The impressive part is how much AI caught on its own,&#8221; he says. &#8220;The important part is how much it missed, and that&#8217;s where the engineer still matters.&#8221;</p>
<p>In a similar manner, ChipAgents just released its new <a href="https://chipagents.ai/research/build-a-chip-bench">build-a-chip-bench</a> tool. It goes from a workload specification to GSD. The company ranked five models. The best, Claude Fable 5.1 achieved a score of 16.6%, whilst the bottom of the pack was GLM-5.3 with a score of zero. These results look dire, but the biggest failure was their lack of understanding of performance constraints.</p>
<p>I believe this will be an even bigger problem when it comes to writing software that has to be cognizant of the underlying hardware and its performance beyond raw speed. Real-time systems deployed within physical AI need predictable latency, at low power, over a broad range of operating conditions. None of this data is available for training today.</p>
<p>I will finish with another comment Vikash made: &#8220;You can automate the work of verification. You cannot automate the responsibility for it.&#8221;</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/putting-ai-to-the-test/">Putting AI To The Test</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Marvell to buy Lumentum; HBM shortages threaten AI expansion; TSMC weighs price hikes; advanced packaging ramps; domain-specialized AI models; Infineon and UMC boost fab investments; Intel's quantum move; Lam and imec advance 3D integration; Google expands TPUs; AI-driven earnings; CPU benchmarking methodology for agentic AI.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/chip-industry-week-in-review-162/">Chip Industry Week In Review</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>

										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><em>Updated 11/2 for Marvell&#8217;s planned acquisition of Lumentum.</em></p>
<h3><strong>Deals and funding</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Marvell</strong> agreed to <a href="https://investor.marvell.com/news-events/press-releases/detail/1040/marvell-and-lumentum-to-combine-creating-a-category-leader-in-ai-connectivity">acquire</a> <strong>Lumentum</strong> in an all-stock transaction with an implied enterprise value of $115B, combining Marvell&#8217;s custom silicon, networking and interconnect technologies with Lumentum&#8217;s lasers, optical transceivers and optical circuit switching. The proposed combination would expand the companies&#8217; capabilities in AI data center connectivity, silicon photonics and advanced packaging. The deal is expected to close in the first half of 2028, subject to shareholder and regulatory approvals.</li>
<li><strong>Intel</strong> plans to <a href="https://www.intc.com/news-events/press-releases/detail/1784/intel-to-acquire-classiq-to-create-a-full-stack-quantum">acquire</a> <strong>Classiq</strong>, a developer of automated quantum algorithm synthesis software. Intel says the acquisition will give it a full stack of quantum offerings, adding to its silicon spin-qubit technology and manufacturing.</li>
<li><strong>Unconventional AI </strong>plans to <a href="https://unconv.ai/blog/unconventional-ai-to-acquire-neureality/">acquire</a> <strong>NeuReality</strong>.</li>
<li><strong>Unconfirmed</strong>:  <strong>Onsemi</strong> reportedly is in <a href="https://www.theinformation.com/articles/chipmaker-onsemi-talks-buy-startup-1-billion">talks to acquire</a> <strong>Ferric</strong>, a startup developing integrated voltage regulators for AI processors, for nearly $1B, according to The Information.</li>
<li><strong>FuriosaAI</strong> raised $620M to scale production of its <a href="https://furiosa.ai/blog/furiosaai-620-million-series-d">AI inference accelerator</a>.</li>
</ul>
<h3 class="p1"><strong>AI memory and capacity</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>SK hynix</strong> expects <a href="https://news.skhynix.com/en/q3-2026-business-results/">memory supply constraints to persist through 2030</a>, with DRAM and HBM volumes effectively sold out through 2029. <strong>Samsung</strong> expects <a href="https://www.mt.co.kr/en/industry/2026/10/29/2026102910520568042?">tight memory supply through 2028</a>, citing lengthy fab construction and equipment lead times, larger dies, and greater process complexity in next-gen HBM, as well as a shift of DRAM capacity toward HBM. Samsung said its 2027 HBM supply is already sold out.</li>
<li><strong>Solidigm</strong>, SK hynix&#8217;s U.S. subsidiary, reportedly is discussing a proposed $45B <a href="https://news.bloomberglaw.com/capital-markets/solidigm-dangles-new-45-billion-us-plant-to-upgrade-china-site">NAND flash</a> fab with the U.S. Commerce Department.</li>
<li class="p3">China’s <strong>CXMT</strong> plans to soon begin limited <a href="https://www.reuters.com/world/china/chinas-cxmt-start-advanced-ai-memory-production-despite-poor-yields-sources-say-2026-10-28/">production of HBM3E memory</a>, despite manufacturing yields of just 20% to 30%, per Reuters.</li>
<li><strong>Anthropic</strong> warned in its IPO filing that <a href="https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/2130357/000162828026067168/anthropicpbc-sx1.htm"><span class="s1">HBM shortages and limited TSMC manufacturing capacity</span></a> could constrain AI expansion.</li>
</ul>
<h3 class="p1"><strong>Advanced packaging </strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>ASE</strong> forecasts a <a href="https://media-aseholdco.todayir.com/20261029160705761664454_en.pdf">doubling of its advanced-packaging</a> revenue in 2027, and <strong>Amkor</strong> is ramping new <a href="https://ir.amkor.com/static-files/70fef79b-6e7c-4f65-91d1-2ee19e575e3c">high-density fan-out and 2.5D</a> packaging programs.</li>
<li><strong>TSMC</strong> reportedly is considering a 20% <a href="https://www.trendforce.com/news/2026/10/27/news-tsmc-reportedly-eyes-20-cowos-price-hike-skips-equipment-price-renegotiations-amid-ai-demand-surge/">price increase for CoWoS</a> as AI demand continues to strain advanced-packaging capacity.</li>
<li><strong>Applied Materials</strong> plans to <a href="https://www.appliedmaterials.com/us/en/newsroom/press-releases/102626-increase-investment-besi-semiconductor.html">increase its stake</a> in <strong>Besi</strong> to ~20%, deepening a strategic partnership that is expanding beyond hybrid bonding into new interconnect architectures and advanced packaging technologies for AI.</li>
</ul>
<h3 class="p1"><strong>Manufacturing </strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Intel Foundry</strong> reported improving <a href="https://stockanalysis.com/stocks/intc/transcripts/714242-q3-2026/">manufacturing execution</a> in Q3, citing better yields and cycle times on 18A. The company also released its 14A 0.9 PDK in October and said it continues to engage prospective external customers around 18A-P and 14A, while advancing its EMIB-T advanced-packaging technology.</li>
<li><strong>Infineon</strong> plans to invest €1.1B through 2034 at its Villach, Austria facility to establish Europe&#8217;s first high-volume manufacturing facility for <a href="https://www.infineon.com/press-release/2026/infpr202610-021">next-gen GaN power</a> semiconductors on 300mm wafers.</li>
<li><b>UMC</b> approved an additional <a href="https://www.umc.com/upload/media/08_Investors/Financials/Quarterly_Results/Quarterly_2020-2029_English_pdf/2026/Q3_2026/UMC26Q3_report.pdf"><span class="s1">$3.2B in capital spending</span></a> to accelerate its capacity expansion in Singapore and Tainan as demand grows for advanced packaging and silicon photonics.</li>
<li><strong>Lam Research</strong> and <strong>imec</strong> expanded their collaboration on advanced <a href="https://newsroom.lamresearch.com/lam-imec-3d-integration-dry-resist-ai-semiconductor-scaling">3D integration, next-gen memory, and high-NA EUV</a> patterning. The work includes deposition, etch, and plasma dicing processes for advanced packaging, along with Lam&#8217;s Aether dry resist technology.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>AI tools and accelerators</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Cadence</strong> introduced a portfolio of domain-specialized <a href="https://newsroom.cadence.com/press-releases/press-release-details/2026/Cadence-Introduces-Maestro-a-Domain-Specialized-AI-Model-Portfolio-to-Revolutionize-Agentic-Chip-and-System-Design/default.aspx">AI models</a> for agentic chip and system design. Integrated with Cadence&#8217;s EDA tools, the portfolio spans multiple model sizes and architectures, combining post-trained LLMs, decision models, and self-improving agent skills.</li>
<li><strong>Arm</strong> introduced a new <a href="https://newsroom.arm.com/news/arm-agi-cpu-agentic-benchmarks">CPU benchmarking methodology for agentic AI</a>. The framework evaluates CPU performance across agentic workloads, including tool execution, orchestration, and reasoning, using repeatable measurements and end-to-end task outcomes.</li>
<li><strong>Google</strong> is expanding the reach of its custom AI accelerators, with <strong>OpenAI</strong> reportedly planning to <a href="https://www.theinformation.com/articles/openai-longtime-nvidia-customer-plans-buy-googles-ai-chips">purchase its TPUs</a>, reports The Information. Separately, <strong>Oracle</strong> will offer Google’s TPUs through its cloud infrastructure beginning in 2027 and plans to add <strong>Arm</strong>’s <a href="https://newsroom.arm.com/news/arm-agi-cpu-oracle-cloud-infrastructure">AGI CPUs</a> in fall 2027.</li>
<li><strong>Broadcom</strong>&#8216;s XPUs and networking equipment will support <strong>Anthropic</strong>&#8216;s AI infrastructure expansion, beginning in 2027, under a <a href="https://investors.broadcom.com/news-releases/news-release-details/bank-america-blackstone-citi-and-morgan-stanley-announce">$63B financing</a> arrangement.</li>
<li><strong>Meta</strong> is expanding deployments of <a href="https://nvidianews.nvidia.com/news/nvidia-and-meta-to-expand-collaboration-on-muse-and-muse-family-of-models-amid-robust-growth">Nvidia</a> and <a href="https://newsroom.amd.com/news/amd-meta-advance-strategic-partnership-power-metas-ai-infrastructure/">AMD</a> AI infrastructure across its data centers, with both chipmakers also taking stakes in the company.</li>
</ul>

<li><strong>Amkor</strong>: Net income $191M, <a href="https://ir.amkor.com/static-files/0ef4c21c-9b2a-4c22-be53-4fd891d17aa0">up 50%</a>, due to a richer mix of <a href="https://ir.amkor.com/static-files/70fef79b-6e7c-4f65-91d1-2ee19e575e3c">high-value advanced packaging</a>.</li>

<li><strong>SK hynix</strong>: Record KRW 104.6T, <a href="https://news.skhynix.com/en/q3-2026-business-results/">up 328%</a>.</li>
<li><strong>Teradyne:</strong> $1.3B, <a href="https://investors.teradyne.com/news-events/press-releases/detail/455/teradyne-reports-third-quarter-2026-results">up 70%</a>, with record memory and systems test revenue.</li>
<li><strong>UMC</strong>: NT$74.2B, <a href="https://www.umc.com/upload/media/08_Investors/Financials/Quarterly_Results/Quarterly_2020-2029_English_pdf/2026/Q3_2026/UMC26Q3_report.pdf">up ~25.6%</a>, with fab utilization reaching 92%.</li>
</ul>

<h2 id="Global"><strong>Global</strong></h2>
<h3><strong>Asia</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Vietnam</strong>&#8216;s first semiconductor fab <a href="https://www.reuters.com/world/asia-pacific/vietnams-first-chip-plant-unlikely-meet-2027-target-sources-say-no-deal-yet-with-2026-10-26/">may miss its 2027 trial-production target</a>, according to Reuters. The estimated US$1.3B Viettel project reportedly has yet to begin construction, while negotiations for ASML lithography equipment remain unresolved. Viettel maintains the project is on schedule.</li>
<li>South Korea&#8217;s <strong>PSK</strong> is planning a new US$125M semiconductor <a href="https://www.thelec.kr/news/articleView.html">equipment manufacturing facility in Yongin&#8217;s</a> semiconductor cluster.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Americas</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>ITIF</strong> says the U.S.-led <a href="https://itif.org/publications/2026/10/27/pax-silica-timeline/">Pax Silica initiative</a> has grown to 26 countries, coordinating on semiconductors, critical minerals, energy, advanced manufacturing, and AI infrastructure to reduce dependence on vulnerable supply chains.</li>
<li><strong>Imec</strong> and <strong>Indiana University</strong> formed a <a href="https://www.imec-int.com/en/press/iu-lands-partnership-international-semiconductor-powerhouse-imec">semiconductor research partnership</a> focused on advanced electronics, photonics, and workforce development. Initial research will test imec-developed electronic and photonic components in extreme environments, including space.</li>
<li><strong>Cadence</strong> will <a href="https://www.cadence.com/en_US/home/company/newsroom/press-releases/pr/2026/cadence-joins-the-genesis-mission-consortium-to-advance-ai.html">provide</a> U.S. <strong>Department of Energy</strong> National Laboratories with access to EDA, SDA, multiphysics simulation, digital twins, advanced packaging, and 3D-IC workflows, along with technical expertise.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Europe</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>SUSS MicroTec</strong> will <a href="https://www.suss.com/ko/news/corporate-news/2026/suss-optimizes-global-production-network-to-support-continued-growth-and-updates-guidance-for-fiscal-year-2026">consolidate semiconductor equipment production</a> in Zhubei, Taiwan, and Sternenfels, Germany, and end manufacturing at its Garching facility near Munich.</li>
</ul>

<h2 id="New Technologies"><strong>New Technologies</strong></h2>
<h3><strong>Chip design and verification</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>ChipAgents</strong> is expanding its <a href="https://chipagents.ai/newsroom/chipagents-ic-star-full-stack-agentic-ai">agentic AI</a> platform across digital and analog chip design with a neural engine that combines persistent design knowledge, advanced reasoning and orchestration, dynamic model routing, and physics-aware models. It will also work with <strong>Nvidia</strong> to fine-tune Nemotron models.</li>
<li><strong>Axiomise</strong> and <strong>Agnisys</strong> teamed up to verify <a href="https://www.agnisys.com/news/agnisys-and-axiomise-collaborate-to-accelerate-noc-design-and-formal-sign-off/">NoCs</a> using formal technologies.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>AI power delivery</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li data-pm-slice="1 1 [&quot;list&quot;,{&quot;spread&quot;:false,&quot;start&quot;:1793,&quot;end&quot;:2245},&quot;regular_list_item&quot;,{&quot;start&quot;:1793,&quot;end&quot;:2245}]"><strong>Infineon</strong> introduced new AI power-delivery technologies, including a <a href="https://www.infineon.com/technology-news/2026/inftn202610-015">±400 V/800 V</a> rack power-distribution reference board, a <a href="https://www.infineon.com/technology-news/2026/inftn202610-016">5.6 kW M-CRPS </a>reference design, and 900 V monolithic bidirectional <a href="https://www.infineon.com/technology-news/2026/inftn202610-014">GaN switches</a> for solid-state transformers in AI data centers.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Compute, memory and photonics</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>PsiQuantum</strong> demonstrated an 8×8 <a href="https://www.psiquantum.com/news-import/psiquantum-unveils-optical-circuit-switch-technology-built-on-advanced-low-loss-silicon-photonics-platform">optical circuit switch</a> built on silicon photonics for AI infrastructure.</li>
<li><strong>TetraMem</strong> taped out a 12nm analog in-memory computing SoC based on multi-level <a href="https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20261027687318/en/TetraMem-Advances-Analog-In-Memory-Computing-with-Tapeout-of-New-12nm-AI-SoC">RRAM</a> and developed using <strong>Synopsys</strong>&#8216; cloud EDA tools.</li>
<li><strong>Fraunhofer IIS</strong> integrated permanent micromagnets directly into the silicon substrate of a <a href="https://www.iis.fraunhofer.de/en/pr/2026/press-release-microbias-integrated-micromagnets.html">magnetic sensor chip</a> at wafer level.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Automotive</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Infineon</strong> introduced a <a href="https://www.infineon.com/technology-news/2026/inftn202610-019">chip-embedding</a> platform for high-voltage power electronics, <a href="https://www.infineon.com/technology-news/2026/inftn202610-018">LinearFET</a> technology for solid-state power distribution in EVs and SDVs, new <a href="https://www.infineon.com/technology-news/2026/inftn202610-020">MOSFETs</a> for 12 V and 48 V vehicle systems, and a <a href="https://www.infineon.com/technology-news/2026/inftn202610-017">high-voltage BMS</a> reference design.</li>
<li><strong>STMicro </strong>released an <a href="https://newsroom.st.com/media-center/press-item.html/stmicroelectronics-introduces-first-automotive-safety-combination-sensors-for-mass-market-applications-p4796-2.html">automotive safety sensor</a> platform that combines accelerometers and gyroscopes for ADAS and vehicle-safety applications.</li>
<li><strong>Bosch</strong> debuted a new <a href="https://www.bosch-semiconductors.com/stories-and-events/smi340-combines-vehicle-safety-and-adas/">MEMS inertial sensor</a> platform, combining ADAS and vehicle-safety functions up to ASIL D.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<h2 id="Research"><strong>Research</strong></h2>
<p data-pm-slice="1 1 []"><strong>TU Munich</strong> researchers developed <a href="https://semiengineering.com/neural-networks-speed-3nm-nanosheet-spice-simulation-tu-munich/">neural-network-based transistor models</a> for 3nm nanosheet devices, accelerating standard-cell library characterization by up to 8× compared with a conventional compact model. The models kept delay-estimation errors below 0.11%.</p>
<p>A <strong>Brown University</strong>-led team demonstrated <a href="https://www.brown.edu/news/2026-10-28/multiferroic-dead-zone">ultrathin multiferroic films</a> that retain ferroelectric and magnetic properties at thicknesses of just a few angstroms, overcoming the dead-layer effect in bismuth ferrite. The films also exhibited altermagnetic properties, potentially enabling low-power memory and logic devices.</p>
<p><strong>Chinese Academy of Sciences</strong>-led researchers developed a <a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41563-026-02780-7">III-nitride-based in-sensor computing device</a> that uses interfacial polarization to separate photogenerated carriers and reduce recombination losses. The sensor-memristor achieved responsivity on the order of 10⁶ A/W, an on/off ratio of 10⁵, and maintained high performance at temperatures up to 300°C.</p>
<p><strong>University of Oxford</strong>-led researchers identified a <a href="https://www.ox.ac.uk/news/2026-10-28-semiconductor-breakthrough-overcomes-major-challenge-in-capturing-solar-energy">pseudo-direct semiconductor in bismuth sulfide</a> that combines strong light absorption with slower electron–hole recombination. Its unusual electronic structure was estimated to reduce recombination rates by about 75% at room temperature compared with conventional semiconductors with similarly strong absorption, potentially improving solar-cell efficiency.</p>
<hr />
<h2 id="Security"><strong>Security</strong></h2>
<p data-pm-slice="1 1 []"><strong>MITRE</strong>’s CWE Program released <a href="https://cwe.mitre.org/data/index.html">CWE 5.0</a>, adding new weaknesses, including <a href="https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/1461.html">Hardware Simulation/Synthesis Mismatch</a>. The new hardware weakness covers security-relevant cases where RTL behaves differently after synthesis, potentially exposing data, altering protected state, or bypassing security controls in the resulting hardware.</p>
<h3><strong>Alerts and hacks</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>TianoCore</strong> disclosed a critical vulnerability in its widely used EDK II UEFI firmware. The <a href="https://github.com/tianocore/edk2/security/advisories/GHSA-99cj-7hg9-mp4m">memory-corruption flaw</a> could allow an unauthenticated network attacker to execute code during PXE boot, before the OS loads and before Secure Boot or measured boot can verify any OS component.</li>
<li><strong>Supermicro</strong> disclosed nine <a href="https://www.supermicro.com/en/support/security_BMC_IPMI_Oct_2026_1">BMC firmware vulnerabilities</a> across two Oct. 26 advisories, including a critical flaw that could let an unauthenticated network attacker execute arbitrary IPMI commands on an active session. The disclosures also cover privilege-escalation, key-exposure, memory-access, and <a href="https://www.supermicro.com/fr-fr/support/security_BMC_IPMI_Oct_2026_2">command-injection</a> flaws.</li>
<li><strong>Keystone</strong> said a breach exposed a key used to <a href="https://teapawt.org/KeystoneWallet/article/2114719318722404578">authenticate its hardware wallets</a>, potentially allowing counterfeit devices to pass authenticity checks. The company is destroying 18,835 unsold devices containing the compromised key.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Government security news</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>NIST</strong> finalized its <a href="https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/ir/8547/final">road map for transitioning to PQC</a>, identifying the quantum-vulnerable standards that federal systems and technology providers will need to replace with quantum-resistant algorithms.</li>
<li>NIST also explained its <a href="https://www.nist.gov/blogs/taking-measure/measurement-research-could-help-devices-tell-you-if-theyve-been-tampered">RF-fingerprinting technique for detecting hardware tampering</a>, using subtle differences in devices’ radio emissions to identify altered or substituted components with more than 95% accuracy in testing.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>New capabilities and tools</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Eclypsium</strong> and <strong>Nvidia</strong> demonstrated how a <a href="https://eclypsium.com/press-release/eclypsium-and-nvidia-collaborate-to-address-emerging-ai-infrastructure-attack-surface-and-strengthen-ai-trust/">compromised privileged component in AI compute</a> infrastructure could alter model weights and outputs during live inference without modifying the stored model.</li>
<li><strong>Altera</strong> introduced its Agilex 2X FPGA and SoC portfolio with a <a href="https://www.altera.com/newsroom/agilex-2x">hardware root of trust, secure boot and attestation</a>, PQC-ready crypto, and line-rate encryption for Ethernet, PCIe and memory.</li>
<li><strong>Google</strong> introduced <a href="https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/models-and-research/gemini-models/introducing-gemini-4-carbon/">Gemini 4 Carbon</a>, its latest frontier model with cybersecurity capabilities.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Security technical papers</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36612">Fault Vulnerability of HW</a> Malware Detectors (<strong>UC Davis</strong> et al)</li>
<li>Detection &amp; classification of <a href="https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00521-026-12467-4">RISC-V HW exploits</a> using ML (<strong>UPC</strong>)</li>
<li>Bringing <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36219">Hardware Security Education</a> to All (<strong>U. of Kansas, UF</strong> et al.)</li>
<li>Provably Secure Data <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37454">Memory-Dependent Prefetching</a> (<strong>DistriNet, KU Leuven</strong>)</li>
<li><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33748">Rowhammer attack</a> on Fil-C&#8217;s software capabilities (Independent researcher and <strong>U. Sussex</strong>)</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<h2 id="Trending Video"><strong>Trending Video</strong></h2>
<p><a href="https://semiengineering.com/why-proof-convergence-matters/">Why Proof Convergence Matters</a>: Leveraging patterns in formal verification to reach sign-off faster. Ashish Darbari, CEO of <strong>Axiomise</strong>, talks about the impact of functional interactions involving safety and security, where to look for common patterns to prevent bugs from creeping into these designs, and how this all can be used to reduce time to sign-off with higher coverage.</p>
<p><iframe src="https://www.youtube.com/embed/EEsCDMSRKoY" width="480" height="270" frameborder="0" allowfullscreen="allowfullscreen"></iframe></p>
<hr />
<h2 id="Quantum Hardware"><strong>Quantum Hardware</strong></h2>
<p><strong>IBM</strong> demonstrated <a href="https://www.ibm.com/quantum/blog/connecting-quantum-processors-with-l-couplers">four interconnects linking two Nighthawk quantum processors</a>, achieving 99% average quantum state-transfer fidelity and 94.75% two-qubit gate fidelity, advancing modular quantum computing.</p>
<p><strong>NVision Quantum</strong> demonstrated a <a href="https://www.nvision-quantum.com/blog/nvision-unveils-first-manufacturable-molecular-qubit">single-molecule spin-photon interface</a> and partnered with HyperLight to integrate molecular qubits onto photonic chips using established semiconductor fabrication processes.</p>
<p><strong>FZ Jülich</strong>-led researchers demonstrated <a href="https://www.fz-juelich.de/en/news/archive/highlights/2026/speed-record-for-quantum-gates">6.8-nanosecond single-qubit gates</a> on a superconducting transmon, achieving 99.981% and 99.989% fidelity. Precision pulse shaping suppressed unwanted transitions, keeping leakage below 0.002% per operation.</p>
<p>A <strong>Technical University of Denmark</strong> team demonstrated integrated <a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-026-78806-0">quantum light sources using 2D materials</a>, coupling WSe₂ single-photon emitters to WS₂ waveguides on a chip, with an estimated minimum waveguide-coupled rate of 1.68 million photons per second.</p>
<hr />
<h2 id="Workforce, Education"><strong>Workforce, Education</strong></h2>
<p data-pm-slice="1 1 []">In a new special report, Semiconductor Engineering examines how <a href="https://semiengineering.com/ai-raises-the-bar-for-tomorrows-engineers/">AI is changing the skills tomorrow’s engineers</a> will need, with universities placing greater emphasis on strong math and physics fundamentals, critical thinking, and the ability to validate AI-generated results. Semiconductor employers say graduates will need both AI fluency and enough technical depth to recognize when AI gets things wrong.</p>
<p data-pm-slice="1 1 []"><strong>Northeastern University</strong> developed a <a href="https://ece.northeastern.edu/news/northeastern-team-earns-second-place-in-ieee-metaverse-grand-challenge-for-vr-and-hologram-based-semiconductor-training/">VR and holographic training platform for semiconductor manufacturing</a> that lets one student operate a simulated nanofabrication furnace used to grow nanowires while other students follow the process on a 3D display.</p>
<p><strong>UCSD</strong> and <strong>TSMC</strong> launched a new <a href="https://today.ucsd.edu/story/uc-san-diego-partners-with-tsmc-to-create-pipeline-from-the-classroom-to-semiconductor-cleanroom">hands-on semiconductor training program</a> for undergraduates.</p>
